Enc.: text encoder (384d = MiniLM-L6-v2, 1024d = BGE-large). $\\Delta$: HS$-$text difference (pp); ``--'' = BA and FPR are not computed for the 1024d comparison because the 384d and 1024d encoders operate at different probe calibration points and do not share a decision threshold. HB: \\checkmark{} = model-level test passes Holm-Bonferroni ($\\alpha{=}0.05$); all 384d model-level tests pass$^\\dagger$. 1024d: HB over 4 model-level precision tests.}\n\\label{tab:hs_vs_text}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe HS advantage is encoder-conditional: against BGE-large (1024d), only OT-7B survives HB correction (\\cref{app:encoder_robustness}). Multi-seed stability appears in \\cref{app:multi_seed}. Against the 384d baseline, HS probes outperform text probes in all four models$^\\dagger$ (\\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}): cross-validated at 7--8B (OT-7B: +21.9pp precision, R1-8B: +20.1pp), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). R1-8B FPR is directionally consistent (5/5 seeds) but not robust ($p{=}0.044$, rank 12/12 in HB step-down; 1/5 seeds Bonferroni; \\cref{app:multi_seed}). R1-32B passes 5/5 seeds but shows a pattern consistent with position mediation (residualized gap NS; \\cref{app:position_residual}); QwQ-32B's position baseline is competitive (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). Overall, 18/20 model-seed combinations pass full HB (\\cref{app:multi_seed}). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 11.1--16.8pp above chance (\\cref{app:control}). A split-half analysis on OT-7B confirms internal validity (\\cref{app:split_half}).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Capacity control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (79--92\\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage across all four models$^\\dagger$ (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$, all HB). The control provides partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Random projection to 384d yields similar results ($\\Delta$Prec +8.3 to +20.5pp; \\cref{app:random_projection}), further weakening the capacity explanation. E5-Mistral-7B (4096d$\\rightarrow$PCA 384d) yields intermediate results (2/4 HB; \\cref{app:e5_mistral}), forming a monotonic encoder-quality gradient with MiniLM (4/4) and BGE-large (1/4).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder robustness.} Against BGE-large~\\citep{xiao2023cpack} (1024d), only OT-7B survives HB (1/4 precision); R1-8B shows adverse FPR direction (+1.2pp). The advantage is encoder-conditional (\\cref{fig:forest_precision}); layer selection (Contribution 1) is encoder-independent.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Position mediation.} Position ($t/T$) accounts for 9--20pp of balanced accuracy,\\footnote{R1-32B's 20.4pp drop reflects $W{=}1$; at $W{\\geq}3$, drops are 9--17pp.} but all models retain non-positional signal above chance (all $p{<}0.001$; \\cref{app:position_residual}). Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage (residualized gap +3.7 to +6.9pp, HB corrected); R1-32B's gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$, $n{=}20$). Adding a quadratic position term $(t/T)^2$ does not change significance conclusions for the 7--8B models. However, QwQ-32B's advantage is fully absorbed by the quadratic term (\\cref{tab:position_quadratic}), suggesting its residualized advantage contains a non-linear position component. Temporal windowing reduces position dependence (24.3pp drop at $W{=}3$ vs.\\ 16.5pp at $W{=}15$).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Caveats.} Nested 5-fold CV confirms the HS precision advantage for both 7--8B models (10/10 folds; \\cref{app:nested_cv}), with shrinkage of 13--25\\% relative to holdout estimates, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. OT-7B nested CV selects L21--27 vs.\\ holdout L16 vs.\\ CR-composite L0; the +21.9pp holdout estimate is an upper bound, and the nested CV shrunk estimate is the conservative reference. This inconsistency itself supports Contribution~1 (metric-induced layer selection bias). R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\\% coverage;\\footnote{R1-32B's BA-optimal layer was identified post-hoc rather than through the pre-registered nested CV protocol used for 7--8B models, further motivating its exploratory status.} at $W{\\geq}3$, dimensionality prevents significance. Regularization $C \\in [0.01, 100]$ yields ${<}2$pp BA variation; decision thresholds are similarly stable (\\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}). QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps): higher precision at later detection. False alarm rates remain 11--50\\%, so current methods characterize the phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors. Per-model details in \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\subsection{32B Model-Specific Analysis}\\label{sec:32b-analysis}\n\nBoth 32B results are exploratory ($n{=}19$--$20$ precludes nested CV, as each fold would contain only 4 traces per class). We report effect sizes as descriptive rather than confirmatory; LOO-CV and multi-seed analyses (\\cref{app:layer_sweep,app:multi_seed}) provide partial robustness checks but do not substitute for independent validation. By contrast, nested CV on the 7--8B models shows 13--25\\% shrinkage (\\cref{app:nested_cv}), confirming those estimates. R1-32B and QwQ-32B share the Qwen-32B architecture (64 layers, 5120 hidden) but present opposite detection profiles (\\cref{tab:32b_divergence}); training method is one plausible differentiator. Replication with $n{\\geq}50$ traces per model is needed before drawing inferential conclusions from 32B results.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Cov.} & \\textbf{Lead (steps)} & \\textbf{HB} & \\textbf{Pos.\\ margin} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-32B & 25\\% & +11.7 to +13.3 & \\checkmark{} ($W{=}1$) & +14.2pp \\\\\nQwQ-32B & 74\\% & $-$1.2 to +1.9 & \\checkmark{} (all $W$) & $-$3.6pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{32B cross-model divergence. R1-32B provides limited early identification with narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides robust significance with poor lead time. HB: \\checkmark{} = passes Holm-Bonferroni ($\\alpha{=}0.05$). Pos.\\ margin: HS BA minus position baseline.}\n\\label{tab:32b_divergence}\n\\end{table}\n\nR1-32B detects commitment 11--13 steps early (+14.2pp over position baseline) but covers only 25\\% of traces ($W{=}1$). QwQ-32B achieves significance at all window sizes with 74\\% coverage but negative HS lead time. QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher BA (0.832 vs.\\ 0.796); HS precision (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) and FPR margins are not significant at $n{=}19$; MDE at 80\\% power is 8.6--9.5pp (\\cref{app:power_analysis}). The divergence suggests per-model validation is needed.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Context}\\label{sec:temporal}\n\nTemporal windowing ($W{>}1$) improves both modalities (\\cref{fig:window_ablation}): at 7--8B, the modality advantage (+20.1 to +21.9pp) exceeds temporal improvement (+13pp). GRU models achieve comparable accuracy but higher FPR (\\cref{tab:gru_multi_seeds,app:temporal_details}).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_forest_precision.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of HS precision advantage over text probes. Blue: vs.\\ MiniLM-384d; orange: vs.\\ BGE-1024d. Solid markers: survives Holm-Bonferroni; hollow: does not. All 384d comparisons are significant (strong to qualified evidence); against BGE-1024d, only OT-7B (strong) survives correction.}\n\\label{fig:forest_precision}\n\\end{figure}\n\nCP-aligned probe score trajectories (\\cref{fig:trajectory} in Appendix) confirm sharper HS transitions at the commitment point, with OT-7B showing the clearest separation and R1-32B the weakest.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\enlargethispage{4\\baselineskip}\n\\subsection{Negative Results}\\label{sec:negative}\n\nNo alternative method consistently matches supervised probing (\\cref{tab:negative}). BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian}/CUSUM fail (0\\% detection on 3/4 models; \\cref{app:bocpd_config}); Mahalanobis distance yields near-zero separation; RepE~\\citep{zou2023representation} is model-dependent (QwQ-32B BA 0.756 vs.\\ R1-32B 0.559); MLP probes overfit; CCS misaligns with temporal structure; cross-model transfer fails. These failures define four requirements: the signal is directional, gradual, model-specific, and linearly separable. Supervised linear probes with principled layer selection form the minimal effective methodology (\\cref{app:negative_details}).\\looseness=-1\n",
```json
      "numLines": 114,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 114
    }
  },
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\citep{gelman2013garden}.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\%), while precision's deep preference dissolves (only 20\% shallow, scattered across layers; \cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry, cross-dataset (AdvBench volatility ratio $1.67\times$; \cref{app:cosine_volatility}), and is internally valid (split-half replication; \cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\looseness=-1
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	Converging evidence supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (\cref{tab:position_quadratic}), so HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\looseness=-1
11	
12	\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}
13	A three-layer ensemble (one probe per threshold-based metric, averaged) provides significant HS advantage for all four models ($p \leq 0.002$, +10.5 to +27.0pp; \cref{tab:ensemble}), eliminating metric pre-selection without improving over oracle single-metric selection.\looseness=-1
14	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	Layer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse conclusions; the multi-metric audit protocol (\cref{sec:discussion}) mitigates this. Contribution~1 generalizes geometrically; Contribution~2 (+15--22pp precision) is cross-validated at 7--8B, exploratory at 32B$^\dagger$, and encoder-conditional; seven alternative methods fail (\cref{tab:negative}).\looseness=-1
4
```

```text
1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{Single dataset.}
4	All probe experiments use one dataset (HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts}); the geometric precondition (shallow-layer compression) is confirmed on AdvBench~\citep{advbench} (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), but CP-dependent claims rely on HarmThoughts, which provides step-level CP annotations derived from manual inspection of reasoning chains. The dataset spans multiple harmful categories (e.g., violence, fraud, illegal activities) but we do not stratify results by category; categories with longer average reasoning chains or earlier CPs may exhibit different probe dynamics. CP positions range from early to late within traces, though the distribution is right-skewed (most CPs occur in the first half of reasoning). Annotation granularity (step-level vs.\ token-level), category distribution, and annotator team composition may all influence which layers carry discriminative signal and how strongly metric bias manifests. Inter-annotator agreement (IAA) was not computed for these annotations. Random annotation noise would attenuate effect sizes symmetrically, making our estimates conservative; systematic noise (e.g., consistent early or late CP placement) could bias layer selection or inflate modality differences. Without IAA, we cannot distinguish these cases, so our conclusions may not transfer to independently annotated corpora. Generalization to other jailbreak datasets, attack taxonomies, or multilingual settings is untested.
5	
6	\paragraph{Model-specific caveats and statistical power.}
7	Current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors: false positive rates remain 11--50\% (model-dependent), and the 76.7\% oracle gap (R1-8B; \cref{sec:oracle}) underscores how much room remains. R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ (25\% coverage, 5/20 traces) and its HS advantage shows a pattern consistent with position mediation (residualized gap +1.9pp, $p{=}0.914$; \cref{app:position_residual}). QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\ 0.796) with negative HS lead time. R1-8B FPR significance is seed-fragile (3/5 HB). At $n{=}19$--$20$, the minimum detectable effect at 80\% power is 8.6--9.5pp (\cref{app:power_analysis}); all 32B conclusions marked $\dagger$ should be interpreted with this constraint.
8	
9	\paragraph{Hyperparameter selection and scope.}
10	Layer (up to 64), $W$ (7 values), $K$ (5 values), and threshold are selected on the same validation set ($n{=}4$--$9$ per-class at 32B, up to $2{,}240$ configurations). The 12-test HB boundary covers final model-metric comparisons but not the full upstream exploration; Type~I error may be underestimated. HB correction covers the final 12 model-metric tests but not the cumulative researcher degrees of freedom in layer, metric, window, and evidence tier selection; cumulative degrees of freedom are substantial (\cref{app:hyperparameter_robustness} provides convergence, cross-validation, and bootstrap analyses addressing this concern). BA-optimal layer indices are sensitive to the train/validation split, spanning 7--98\% depth across models and splits (\cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}); for example, OT-7B shifts from L16 to L2 and QwQ-32B from L60 to L63 between 70/30 and 60/20/20 splits, a direct manifestation of the layer selection sensitivity that constitutes our primary finding. Threshold-based metrics are more stable but still vary by up to 4 layers. $K{=}5$ was selected on the R1-8B validation set and held fixed (\cref{app:k_ablation}); joint (layer, $W$) stability was verified for QwQ-32B (\cref{app:layer_sweep}) but not R1-32B. CP annotations follow HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts} without independent IAA evaluation; if annotation error is ${\geq}2$ steps, QwQ-32B's lead time ($-$1.2 to +1.9) falls within noise, though perturbation analysis shows smooth degradation with no cliff effects (\cref{app:cp_perturbation}). Three of four models share the Qwen architecture family; observed patterns may reflect Qwen-specific representation properties rather than universal reasoning-model characteristics. Only R1-8B (Llama-based) provides cross-architecture evidence. We cover 2 architectures and 2 scales; whether metric-induced layer selection bias generalizes to non-reasoning LLMs or encoder-only architectures remains an open question. Intermediate scales, diverse base architectures, proprietary models, and multilingual settings are untested. Results are correlational; causal intervention is needed to confirm mechanistic involvement. Cross-model transfer was tested with PCA-based subspace alignment only; more sophisticated approaches (e.g., Procrustes rotation, CKA-guided projection~\citep{kornblith2019cka}, model stitching~\citep{bansal2021stitching}) remain future work.
11	
12	\paragraph{CP permanence assumption.}
13	Our binary CP definition assumes that once safety deliberation ceases, it does not resume. In practice, some reasoning traces exhibit oscillating patterns where the model intermittently returns to safety-aware reasoning after apparent commitment. The current binary framework treats the first permanent cessation as CP, but traces with oscillating safety deliberation violate this assumption and may be misclassified. A multi-state formulation (e.g., pre-commitment / wavering / committed) could better capture these dynamics but would require richer annotations and larger datasets to train reliably.
14	
15	\section*{Ethics Statement}
16	This work analyzes safety-relevant reasoning traces to characterize probe behavior, not to circumvent safety mechanisms. All experiments use publicly available models and datasets (HarmThoughts, AdvBench). No human subjects were involved.
17	
```

```text
31	
32	\section{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}
33	\label{app:checklist}
34	
35	We address the ARR Responsible NLP Research requirements below.
36	
37	\begin{itemize}
38	    \item \textbf{Limitations discussed:} Yes (\cref{sec:limitations}).
39	    \item \textbf{Potential risks:} This research analyzes jailbreak traces to understand how reasoning models commit to generating harmful content. We do not develop new attack methods or release jailbreak prompts. All analyzed content comes from the publicly available HarmThoughts benchmark. We acknowledge a dual-use concern: safety probes that detect commitment could in principle be used to craft reasoning traces that evade detection. We believe the defensive value of understanding commitment dynamics outweighs this risk, as evasion requires white-box access to the probe, which a deployed monitoring system would not expose.
40	    \item \textbf{Computational budget:} Our experiments involve 4 models with 209, 228, 100, and 95 traces respectively (632 total), using linear probes and single-layer GRUs. Total compute is minimal (under 4 GPU-hours on a single RTX A6000). No large-scale pretraining or finetuning was performed.
41	    \item \textbf{Data:} We use HarmThoughts, a publicly available benchmark with step-level safety annotations, and AdvBench~\citep{advbench} for cross-dataset geometric validation. No new data collection was performed.
42	    \item \textbf{Human subjects:} No human subjects research was conducted.
43	    \item \textbf{Reproducibility:} All hyperparameters are reported in \cref{app:gru_details,app:bocpd_config}. Code and corrected data will be released upon publication.
44	\end{itemize}
45	
46	
47	\section{Layer Sweep Results}
48	\label{app:layer_sweep}
49	
50	For each model, we sweep all transformer layers and evaluate five candidate selection metrics: (1)~balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate (the CR-composite), (2)~pure balanced accuracy, (3)~precision, (4)~inverse pre-CP FPR, and (5)~an additive composite. The CR-composite systematically selects shallow layers across all four models (including R1-8B under the full L0--L24 sweep; \cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}) because early-layer representations exhibit higher crossing rates (more frequent threshold crossings) regardless of discriminative power. Three threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) converge on deep layers for all models; pure balanced accuracy selects layers whose depth varies with the data split (under 70/30: L30 for QwQ-32B, L47 for R1-32B; under the canonical 60/20/20: L63 and L46 respectively; \cref{tab:ba_layer_results}).
51	
52	\paragraph{Selected layers.} R1-8B: under the original L12--L24 sweep, all five metrics agreed on L14 (44\%); extending to L0--L24, crossing rate shifts to L0 while threshold-based metrics retain L14, a 16-layer gap (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). OT-7B: CR-composite selects L0 (0\%), threshold-based consensus selects L16 (57\%). QwQ-32B: CR-composite selects L2 (3\%), threshold-based consensus selects L63 (98\%). R1-32B: CR-composite selects L10 (16\%), threshold-based consensus selects L63 (98\%). The disagreement is most extreme for 64-layer models, where the CR-composite selects layers in the bottom 16\% of the network while threshold-based metrics select layers at 98\% depth. Under 70/30, pure balanced accuracy selects L30 (47\%) for QwQ-32B and L47 (73\%) for R1-32B; under the canonical 60/20/20, it selects L63 (98\%) and L46 (72\%), which demonstrates sensitivity to the data split.
53	
54	\paragraph{Impact on modality comparison.} At the CR-composite layers, HS vs.\ text precision $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ-32B), both non-significant. At the threshold-based deep layers, all $p$-values fall below 0.005. The initially reported ``scale-dependent'' pattern (HS advantage at 7--8B but not 32B) was entirely an artifact of layer selection methodology, not a genuine property of model scale (\cref{sec:layer-results}).
55	
56	\paragraph{Layer selection stability (LOO-CV).} Leave-one-out cross-validation on the validation set shows moderate consistency for 7--8B models (65--68\% dominant-layer agreement) but lower for 32B, confirming that layer selection instability is particularly acute at small sample sizes. Under pure balanced accuracy, the dominant layer is selected in 68\% (QwQ-32B: L30, 13/19 folds) and 65\% (R1-32B: L47, 13/20 folds) of folds. Alternative selections scatter across neighboring layers (L29, L55--56 for QwQ; L32, L38, L43 for R1-32B), reinforcing the need for multi-metric auditing rather than single-metric layer selection. Threshold-based metrics show tighter convergence to L63 for both 32B models. Expanding the criterion from top-1 to top-3 layers does not change conclusions: the top-3 BA-selected layers for QwQ-32B (L30, L29, L55) and R1-32B (L47, L43, L38) all fall in the mid-to-deep range, and threshold-based metrics remain converged at L63 across all folds.
57	
58	\paragraph{Joint (layer, $W$) sensitivity.} Under the original 70/30 validation protocol, we evaluate QwQ-32B across 64 layers $\times$ 3 windows ($W \in \{1, 3, 5\}$) (192 configurations) (\cref{tab:joint_layer_w}). L60 is near-optimal: within 2.1pp of the global best at $W{=}3$ and the top-ranked layer at $W{=}5$. (Under 60/20/20, threshold-based metrics select L63; the shift from L60 to L63 across splits itself illustrates layer selection sensitivity.) The optimal layer shifts from middle-deep (L46--47 at $W{=}1$) to deep (L60 at $W{=}5$), which confirms a layer-window interaction. L30, selected by per-fold LOO-CV at $W{=}1$ under balanced accuracy, is suboptimal across all windows (max BA $= 0.769$ vs.\ global best 0.817). Joint (layer, $W$) stability was evaluated via grid search on $W \in \{1, 3, 5\}$ for QwQ-32B only; full leave-one-fold-out validation of the (layer, $W$) pair and extension to R1-32B remain for future work.
59	
60	\begin{table}[h]
61	\centering
62	\small
63	\caption{QwQ-32B joint (layer, $W$) grid search under 70/30 validation. L60 is near-optimal at recommended windows ($W{=}3$--$5$). L30 (LOO-CV selection at $W{=}1$) is suboptimal across all windows. Under the canonical 60/20/20 split, threshold-based metrics select L63.}
64	\label{tab:joint_layer_w}
65	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
66	\begin{tabular}{clccc}
67	\toprule
68	$W$ & \textbf{Best layer} & \textbf{Best BA} & \textbf{L60 BA} & \textbf{L60 rank} \\
69	\midrule
70	1 & L47 & 0.798 & 0.783 & $\sim$15th \\
71	3 & L46 & 0.817 & 0.796 & $\sim$6th \\
72	5 & L60 & 0.808 & 0.808 & 1st \\
73	\bottomrule
74	\end{tabular}}
75	\end{table}
76	
77	\paragraph{R1-8B full-layer sweep.}
78	\label{app:r18b_full_layer}
79	The original R1-8B layer sweep covered L12--L24 (13 layers), where all five metrics agreed on L14 (\cref{tab:layer_selection}). Extending to L0--L24 reveals that crossing rate consistently selects L0 across all window sizes, while threshold-based metrics retain mid-to-deep layers (L12--L16 for $W{=}1$--$5$; L9 at $W{=}15$, marginal $\Delta{=}0.003$ between shallow and deep means)---a 9--16 layer divergence (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). Unlike 32B models, the bias is isolated to crossing rate; threshold-based metrics show no shallow shift for R1-8B. This confirms the crossing-rate bias generalizes to Llama-architecture models. HS probes maintain significant advantage across all 25 layers and window sizes (precision gap ${\sim}$0.15--0.17), unlike OT-7B and 32B models where significance drops at shallow layers.
80	
81	\begin{table}[h]
82	\centering
83	\small
84	\caption{R1-8B full-layer sweep (L0--L24, extending the original L12--L24 range). Best layer selected by crossing rate vs.\ threshold-based metrics across four window sizes. Score = balanced accuracy at the threshold-based layer. Layer gap = crossing-rate layer minus threshold-based layer.}
85	\label{tab:r18b_full_layer}
86	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
87	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
88	\toprule
89	& $W{=}1$ & $W{=}3$ & $W{=}5$ & $W{=}15$ \\
90	\midrule
91	Crossing rate & L0 & L0 & L0 & L0 \\
92	Threshold (composite) & L16 (0.789) & L12 (0.796) & L12 (0.803) & L9 (0.784) \\
93	\midrule
94	Layer gap & 16 & 12 & 12 & 9 \\
95	\bottomrule
96	\end{tabular}}
97	\end{table}
98	
99	\subsection{Random-Label Decomposition Control}
100	\label{app:random_cp}
101	
102	To decompose the sources of metric disagreement in layer selection, we replace the true CP annotations with uniformly random labels and re-run the full layer sweep on QwQ-32B (95 traces, 64 layers, 10 random-label repeats). \cref{tab:random_cp} reports the fraction of repeats selecting a shallow layer (L$\leq$10) and the mean selected layer.
103	
104	\begin{table}[h]
105	\centering
106	\small
107	\caption{QwQ-32B layer selection under random CP labels (10 repeats). Shallow\%: fraction selecting a layer L$\leq$10. Real-CP layer shown for reference.}
108	\label{tab:random_cp}
109	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
110	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
111	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
112	\toprule
113	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Real-CP} & \textbf{Shallow\%} & \textbf{Rand.\ mean layer} \\
114	\midrule
115	Crossing rate & L4 & 100\% & $3.4 \pm 2.6$ \\
116	CR-composite & L4 & 80\% & $13.1 \pm 18.1$ \\
117	Precision & L63 & 20\% & $33.8 \pm 19.8$ \\
118	Inv.\ FPR & L62 & 10\% & $36.4 \pm 17.0$ \\
119	Bal.\ accuracy & L63 & 20\% & $33.3 \pm 20.3$ \\
120	\bottomrule
121	\end{tabular}}
122	\end{table}
123	
124	The results decompose the metric disagreement into two components. (A)~The crossing-rate shallow-layer preference is label-independent: 100\% of random-label repeats still select layers L0--L7, confirming that this bias reflects representation geometry (shallow-layer cosine distance compression; \cref{app:cosine_volatility}) rather than any property of the safety labels. The CR-composite inherits this bias (80\% shallow). (B)~Threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR) select deep layers only with real safety labels; under random labels, selections scatter across the full layer range (mean L34--L36, high variance). This confirms that deep-layer probe performance reflects genuine safety-relevant structure. The full metric disagreement (L4 vs.\ L63, a 59-layer gap) is the superposition of a geometric artifact on the crossing-rate side and a real safety signal on the threshold-based side.
125	
126	
127	\section{Representation Compression Analysis}
128	\label{app:cosine_volatility}
129	
130	We measure step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) vs.\ threshold-based (deep) layers across all models to characterize the compression mechanism underlying the crossing rate bias (\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}).
131	
132	\begin{table}[h]
133	\centering
134	\small
135	\caption{Step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) and threshold-based (deep) layers. Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations (1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller inter-step distances).}
136	\label{tab:cosine_volatility}
137	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
138	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
139	\toprule
140	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Layer (type)} & \textbf{Mean} & \textbf{Std} \\
141	\midrule
142	R1-8B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.186 & 0.097 \\
143	 & L14 (thresh.) & 0.411 & 0.143 \\[2pt]
144	OT-7B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.077 & 0.080 \\
145	 & L16 (thresh.) & 0.206 & 0.091 \\[2pt]
146	QwQ-32B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.037 & 0.064 \\
147	 & L63 (thresh.) & 0.084 & 0.056 \\[2pt]
148	R1-32B & L10 (CR-comp.) & 0.067 & 0.050 \\
149	 & L63 (thresh.) & 0.092 & 0.076 \\
150	\bottomrule
151	\end{tabular}}
152	\end{table}
153	
154	Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations: consecutive steps cluster within small cosine distances (0.04--0.19), while deep-layer representations spread more widely (0.09--0.41). Inter-step distances at shallow layers are 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models. A linear probe operating in this compressed space sits near the noise floor, where minor numerical fluctuations produce spurious threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Deep layers produce well-separated representations where crossings correspond to genuine semantic transitions.
155	
156	\paragraph{Cross-dataset evidence.}
157	To assess whether this geometric precondition generalizes beyond HarmThoughts, we compute the same shallow/deep volatility ratio on AdvBench~\citep{advbench} traces using R1-8B. The deep-layer mean cosine distance (0.442) is $1.67\times$ the shallow-layer mean (0.265), consistent with the 1.4--2.7$\times$ range observed across all four models on HarmThoughts (\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}). This confirms that the representational compression underlying metric-induced layer selection bias is not dataset-specific and does not depend on CP annotations.
158	
159	\subsection{AdvBench Boundary Condition}
160	\label{app:advbench_cp}
161	
162	We analyze R1-8B behavior on 388 substantive-response AdvBench~\citep{advbench} traces (of 520 total) to characterize a boundary condition for the CP framework (\cref{tab:advbench_boundary}).
163	
164	\begin{table}[h]
165	\centering
166	\small
167	\caption{R1-8B behavior on AdvBench adversarial prompts (388 substantive-response traces of 520 total). CP${=}0$ indicates the model produces harmful content from the first reasoning step without prior safety deliberation.}
168	\label{tab:advbench_boundary}
169	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
170	\begin{tabular}{lc}
171	\toprule
172	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Value} \\
173	\midrule
174	Jailbreak rate & 13.4\% \\
175	CP${=}0$ (among JB) & 90.4\% \\
176	Mean CP position (among JB) & 1.4 steps \\
177	\bottomrule
178	\end{tabular}}
179	\end{table}
180	
181	Adversarial prompts designed to bypass safety mechanisms (e.g., GCG suffixes) cause the model to skip safety deliberation entirely: 90.4\% of jailbroken traces have CP${=}0$, meaning the model commits to harmful generation from the first reasoning step. This contrasts with HarmThoughts, where non-adversarial prompts trigger extended safety deliberation before commitment (mean relative CP position ${\approx}0.7$). The CP framework is most informative when prompts elicit deliberation that can be monitored; adversarial prompts that circumvent deliberation represent a boundary condition where commitment detection is vacuous. The geometric compression evidence (\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}; volatility ratio $1.67\times$) remains valid because it measures representational properties independent of CP annotations.
182	
183	\section{Holm-Bonferroni Step-Down Correction}
184	\label{app:holm_bonferroni}
185	
186	\cref{tab:holm_bonferroni} reports the full test battery used to assess hidden-state superiority over text probes. Because balanced accuracy, precision, and FPR share the same confusion matrix, the three per-model metrics are correlated; the effective number of independent comparisons is four (one per model). We report both the conservative 12-test correction and the 4-test model-level correction: both yield the same outcome (all tests pass at $\alpha{=}0.05$). Under 4-test HB, the tightest threshold falls on R1-8B FPR ($p{=}0.044$, rank-4 threshold $\alpha/1{=}0.050$), which still passes. Tests are ranked by $p$-value; each test $i$ (rank $i$ of $m$) must satisfy $p_i < \alpha / (m - i + 1)$ under the step-down procedure.
187	
188	\begin{table}[h]
189	\centering
190	\small
191	\caption{Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction for 12 tests (4 models $\times$ 3 correlated metrics). Each test uses a two-sided null-centered bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) on trace-level metric differences (HS minus text). All 12 pass at $\alpha{=}0.05$; a 4-test model-level correction (one per model, using the worst $p$-value) also passes.}
192	\label{tab:holm_bonferroni}
193	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
194	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
195	\begin{tabular}{clllcc}
196	\toprule
197	\textbf{Rank} & \textbf{Model} & \textbf{Metric} & \textbf{$p$-val} & \textbf{Thresh.} & \textbf{Pass} \\
198	\midrule
199	1  & R1-8B  & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0042 & \checkmark \\
200	2  & OT-7B  & Bal.\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0045 & \checkmark \\
```

```json
  "promptId": "8fcde8eb-3062-4e4b-9261-fd041443edf6",

  "uuid": "30550539-cfd2-4abb-98b0-ba6166d68e72",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "type": "text",
    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex",
      "content": "\\appendix\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Appendix Contents:}\n\\cref{app:checklist}~Responsible NLP Research Checklist;\n\\cref{app:layer_sweep}~Layer Sweep Results;\n\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}~Representation Compression Analysis;\n\\cref{app:holm_bonferroni}~Holm-Bonferroni Correction;\n\\cref{app:window_ablation}~Window Size Ablation;\n\\cref{app:ot7b_details}~OpenThinker-7B Details;\n\\cref{app:gru_details}~GRU Training Details;\n\\cref{app:control}~Control Task Methodology;\n\\cref{sec:oracle}~Oracle Upper Bound;\n\\cref{app:cp_perturbation}~CP Annotation Perturbation;\n\\cref{app:bocpd_config}~BOCPD/CUSUM Configuration;\n\\cref{app:k_ablation}~Detection Threshold Ablation;\n\\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}~Decision Threshold Sensitivity;\n\\cref{app:bug}~M002 Bug Disclosure;\n\\cref{app:two_stage}~Adaptive Two-Stage Detection;\n\\cref{app:temporal_details}~Temporal Ablation Details;\n\\cref{app:negative_details}~Negative Results Details (incl.\\ \\cref{tab:negative});\n\\cref{app:transfer_details}~Cross-Model Transfer Details;\n\\cref{app:encoder_robustness}~Encoder Robustness;\n\\cref{app:random_projection}~Random Projection Baseline;\n\\cref{app:e5_mistral}~LLM-Based Text Encoder;\n\\cref{app:position_residual}~Position-Residualized Probing;\n\\cref{app:hyperparameter_robustness}~Hyperparameter Robustness;\n\\cref{app:multi_seed}~Multi-Seed Stability;\n\\cref{app:split_half}~Split-Half Internal Validity;\n\\cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}~BA-Layer Modality Comparison;\n\\cref{app:power_analysis}~Post-Hoc Power Analysis.\n\n\\section{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}\n\\label{app:checklist}\n\nWe address the ARR Responsible NLP Research requirements below.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Limitations discussed:} Yes (\\cref{sec:limitations}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Potential risks:} This research analyzes jailbreak traces to understand how reasoning models commit to generating harmful content. We do not develop new attack methods or release jailbreak prompts. All analyzed content comes from the publicly available HarmThoughts benchmark. We acknowledge a dual-use concern: safety probes that detect commitment could in principle be used to craft reasoning traces that evade detection. We believe the defensive value of understanding commitment dynamics outweighs this risk, as evasion requires white-box access to the probe, which a deployed monitoring system would not expose.\n    \\item \\textbf{Computational budget:} Our experiments involve 4 models with 209, 228, 100, and 95 traces respectively (632 total), using linear probes and single-layer GRUs. Total compute is minimal (under 4 GPU-hours on a single RTX A6000). No large-scale pretraining or finetuning was performed.\n    \\item \\textbf{Data:} We use HarmThoughts, a publicly available benchmark with step-level safety annotations, and AdvBench~\\citep{advbench} for cross-dataset geometric validation. No new data collection was performed.\n    \\item \\textbf{Human subjects:} No human subjects research was conducted.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reproducibility:} All hyperparameters are reported in \\cref{app:gru_details,app:bocpd_config}. Code and corrected data will be released upon publication.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\n\\section{Layer Sweep Results}\n\\label{app:layer_sweep}\n\nFor each model, we sweep all transformer layers and evaluate five candidate selection metrics: (1)~balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate (the CR-composite), (2)~pure balanced accuracy, (3)~precision, (4)~inverse pre-CP FPR, and (5)~an additive composite. The CR-composite systematically selects shallow layers across all four models (including R1-8B under the full L0--L24 sweep; \\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}) because early-layer representations exhibit higher crossing rates (more frequent threshold crossings) regardless of discriminative power. Three threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) converge on deep layers for all models; pure balanced accuracy selects layers whose depth varies with the data split (under 70/30: L30 for QwQ-32B, L47 for R1-32B; under the canonical 60/20/20: L63 and L46 respectively; \\cref{tab:ba_layer_results}).\n\n\\paragraph{Selected layers.} R1-8B: under the original L12--L24 sweep, all five metrics agreed on L14 (44\\%); extending to L0--L24, crossing rate shifts to L0 while threshold-based metrics retain L14, a 16-layer gap (\\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). OT-7B: CR-composite selects L0 (0\\%), threshold-based consensus selects L16 (57\\%). QwQ-32B: CR-composite selects L2 (3\\%), threshold-based consensus selects L63 (98\\%). R1-32B: CR-composite selects L10 (16\\%), threshold-based consensus selects L63 (98\\%). The disagreement is most extreme for 64-layer models, where the CR-composite selects layers in the bottom 16\\% of the network while threshold-based metrics select layers at 98\\% depth. Under 70/30, pure balanced accuracy selects L30 (47\\%) for QwQ-32B and L47 (73\\%) for R1-32B; under the canonical 60/20/20, it selects L63 (98\\%) and L46 (72\\%), which demonstrates sensitivity to the data split.\n\n\\paragraph{Impact on modality comparison.} At the CR-composite layers, HS vs.\\ text precision $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ-32B), both non-significant. At the threshold-based deep layers, all $p$-values fall below 0.005. The initially reported ``scale-dependent'' pattern (HS advantage at 7--8B but not 32B) was entirely an artifact of layer selection methodology, not a genuine property of model scale (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n\\paragraph{Layer selection stability (LOO-CV).} Leave-one-out cross-validation on the validation set shows moderate consistency for 7--8B models (65--68\\% dominant-layer agreement) but lower for 32B, confirming that layer selection instability is particularly acute at small sample sizes. Under pure balanced accuracy, the dominant layer is selected in 68\\% (QwQ-32B: L30, 13/19 folds) and 65\\% (R1-32B: L47, 13/20 folds) of folds. Alternative selections scatter across neighboring layers (L29, L55--56 for QwQ; L32, L38, L43 for R1-32B), reinforcing the need for multi-metric auditing rather than single-metric layer selection. Threshold-based metrics show tighter convergence to L63 for both 32B models. Expanding the criterion from top-1 to top-3 layers does not change conclusions: the top-3 BA-selected layers for QwQ-32B (L30, L29, L55) and R1-32B (L47, L43, L38) all fall in the mid-to-deep range, and threshold-based metrics remain converged at L63 across all folds.\n\n\\paragraph{Joint (layer, $W$) sensitivity.} Under the original 70/30 validation protocol, we evaluate QwQ-32B across 64 layers $\\times$ 3 windows ($W \\in \\{1, 3, 5\\}$) (192 configurations) (\\cref{tab:joint_layer_w}). L60 is near-optimal: within 2.1pp of the global best at $W{=}3$ and the top-ranked layer at $W{=}5$. (Under 60/20/20, threshold-based metrics select L63; the shift from L60 to L63 across splits itself illustrates layer selection sensitivity.) The optimal layer shifts from middle-deep (L46--47 at $W{=}1$) to deep (L60 at $W{=}5$), which confirms a layer-window interaction. L30, selected by per-fold LOO-CV at $W{=}1$ under balanced accuracy, is suboptimal across all windows (max BA $= 0.769$ vs.\\ global best 0.817). Joint (layer, $W$) stability was evaluated via grid search on $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5\\}$ for QwQ-32B only; full leave-one-fold-out validation of the (layer, $W$) pair and extension to R1-32B remain for future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{QwQ-32B joint (layer, $W$) grid search under 70/30 validation. L60 is near-optimal at recommended windows ($W{=}3$--$5$). L30 (LOO-CV selection at $W{=}1$) is suboptimal across all windows. Under the canonical 60/20/20 split, threshold-based metrics select L63.}\n\\label{tab:joint_layer_w}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{clccc}\n\\toprule\n$W$ & \\textbf{Best layer} & \\textbf{Best BA} & \\textbf{L60 BA} & \\textbf{L60 rank} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & L47 & 0.798 & 0.783 & $\\sim$15th \\\\\n3 & L46 & 0.817 & 0.796 & $\\sim$6th \\\\\n5 & L60 & 0.808 & 0.808 & 1st \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{R1-8B full-layer sweep.}\n\\label{app:r18b_full_layer}\nThe original R1-8B layer sweep covered L12--L24 (13 layers), where all five metrics agreed on L14 (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). Extending to L0--L24 reveals that crossing rate consistently selects L0 across all window sizes, while threshold-based metrics retain mid-to-deep layers (L12--L16 for $W{=}1$--$5$; L9 at $W{=}15$, marginal $\\Delta{=}0.003$ between shallow and deep means)---a 9--16 layer divergence (\\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). Unlike 32B models, the bias is isolated to crossing rate; threshold-based metrics show no shallow shift for R1-8B. This confirms the crossing-rate bias generalizes to Llama-architecture models. HS probes maintain significant advantage across all 25 layers and window sizes (precision gap ${\\sim}$0.15--0.17), unlike OT-7B and 32B models where significance drops at shallow layers.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{R1-8B full-layer sweep (L0--L24, extending the original L12--L24 range). Best layer selected by crossing rate vs.\\ threshold-based metrics across four window sizes. Score = balanced accuracy at the threshold-based layer. Layer gap = crossing-rate layer minus threshold-based layer.}\n\\label{tab:r18b_full_layer}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n& $W{=}1$ & $W{=}3$ & $W{=}5$ & $W{=}15$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCrossing rate & L0 & L0 & L0 & L0 \\\\\nThreshold (composite) & L16 (0.789) & L12 (0.796) & L12 (0.803) & L9 (0.784) \\\\\n\\midrule\nLayer gap & 16 & 12 & 12 & 9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Random-Label Decomposition Control}\n\\label{app:random_cp}\n\nTo decompose the sources of metric disagreement in layer selection, we replace the true CP annotations with uniformly random labels and re-run the full layer sweep on QwQ-32B (95 traces, 64 layers, 10 random-label repeats). \\cref{tab:random_cp} reports the fraction of repeats selecting a shallow layer (L$\\leq$10) and the mean selected layer.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{QwQ-32B layer selection under random CP labels (10 repeats). Shallow\\%: fraction selecting a layer L$\\leq$10. Real-CP layer shown for reference.}\n\\label{tab:random_cp}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Real-CP} & \\textbf{Shallow\\%} & \\textbf{Rand.\\ mean layer} \\\\\n\\midrule\nCrossing rate & L4 & 100\\% & $3.4 \\pm 2.6$ \\\\\nCR-composite & L4 & 80\\% & $13.1 \\pm 18.1$ \\\\\nPrecision & L63 & 20\\% & $33.8 \\pm 19.8$ \\\\\nInv.\\ FPR & L62 & 10\\% & $36.4 \\pm 17.0$ \\\\\nBal.\\ accuracy & L63 & 20\\% & $33.3 \\pm 20.3$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe results decompose the metric disagreement into two components. (A)~The crossing-rate shallow-layer preference is label-independent: 100\\% of random-label repeats still select layers L0--L7, confirming that this bias reflects representation geometry (shallow-layer cosine distance compression; \\cref{app:cosine_volatility}) rather than any property of the safety labels. The CR-composite inherits this bias (80\\% shallow). (B)~Threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR) select deep layers only with real safety labels; under random labels, selections scatter across the full layer range (mean L34--L36, high variance). This confirms that deep-layer probe performance reflects genuine safety-relevant structure. The full metric disagreement (L4 vs.\\ L63, a 59-layer gap) is the superposition of a geometric artifact on the crossing-rate side and a real safety signal on the threshold-based side.\n\n\n\\section{Representation Compression Analysis}\n\\label{app:cosine_volatility}\n\nWe measure step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) vs.\\ threshold-based (deep) layers across all models to characterize the compression mechanism underlying the crossing rate bias (\\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) and threshold-based (deep) layers. Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations (1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller inter-step distances).}\n\\label{tab:cosine_volatility}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Layer (type)} & \\textbf{Mean} & \\textbf{Std} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.186 & 0.097 \\\\\n & L14 (thresh.) & 0.411 & 0.143 \\\\[2pt]\nOT-7B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.077 & 0.080 \\\\\n & L16 (thresh.) & 0.206 & 0.091 \\\\[2pt]\nQwQ-32B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.037 & 0.064 \\\\\n & L63 (thresh.) & 0.084 & 0.056 \\\\[2pt]\nR1-32B & L10 (CR-comp.) & 0.067 & 0.050 \\\\\n & L63 (thresh.) & 0.092 & 0.076 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nShallow layers produce highly compressed representations: consecutive steps cluster within small cosine distances (0.04--0.19), while deep-layer representations spread more widely (0.09--0.41). Inter-step distances at shallow layers are 1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models. A linear probe operating in this compressed space sits near the noise floor, where minor numerical fluctuations produce spurious threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Deep layers produce well-separated representations where crossings correspond to genuine semantic transitions.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset evidence.}\nTo assess whether this geometric precondition generalizes beyond HarmThoughts, we compute the same shallow/deep volatility ratio on AdvBench~\\citep{advbench} traces using R1-8B. The deep-layer mean cosine distance (0.442) is $1.67\\times$ the shallow-layer mean (0.265), consistent with the 1.4--2.7$\\times$ range observed across all four models on HarmThoughts (\\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}). This confirms that the representational compression underlying metric-induced layer selection bias is not dataset-specific and does not depend on CP annotations.\n\n\\subsection{AdvBench Boundary Condition}\n\\label{app:advbench_cp}\n\nWe analyze R1-8B behavior on 388 substantive-response AdvBench~\\citep{advbench} traces (of 520 total) to characterize a boundary condition for the CP framework (\\cref{tab:advbench_boundary}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{R1-8B behavior on AdvBench adversarial prompts (388 substantive-response traces of 520 total). CP${=}0$ indicates the model produces harmful content from the first reasoning step without prior safety deliberation.}\n\\label{tab:advbench_boundary}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Value} \\\\\n\\midrule\nJailbreak rate & 13.4\\% \\\\\nCP${=}0$ (among JB) & 90.4\\% \\\\\nMean CP position (among JB) & 1.4 steps \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nAdversarial prompts designed to bypass safety mechanisms (e.g., GCG suffixes) cause the model to skip safety deliberation entirely: 90.4\\% of jailbroken traces have CP${=}0$, meaning the model commits to harmful generation from the first reasoning step. This contrasts with HarmThoughts, where non-adversarial prompts trigger extended safety deliberation before commitment (mean relative CP position ${\\approx}0.7$). The CP framework is most informative when prompts elicit deliberation that can be monitored; adversarial prompts that circumvent deliberation represent a boundary condition where commitment detection is vacuous. The geometric compression evidence (\\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}; volatility ratio $1.67\\times$) remains valid because it measures representational properties independent of CP annotations.\n\n\\section{Holm-Bonferroni Step-Down Correction}\n\\label{app:holm_bonferroni}\n\n\\cref{tab:holm_bonferroni} reports the full test battery used to assess hidden-state superiority over text probes. Because balanced accuracy, precision, and FPR share the same confusion matrix, the three per-model metrics are correlated; the effective number of independent comparisons is four (one per model). We report both the conservative 12-test correction and the 4-test model-level correction: both yield the same outcome (all tests pass at $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Under 4-test HB, the tightest threshold falls on R1-8B FPR ($p{=}0.044$, rank-4 threshold $\\alpha/1{=}0.050$), which still passes. Tests are ranked by $p$-value; each test $i$ (rank $i$ of $m$) must satisfy $p_i < \\alpha / (m - i + 1)$ under the step-down procedure.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction for 12 tests (4 models $\\times$ 3 correlated metrics). Each test uses a two-sided null-centered bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) on trace-level metric differences (HS minus text). All 12 pass at $\\alpha{=}0.05$; a 4-test model-level correction (one per model, using the worst $p$-value) also passes.}\n\\label{tab:holm_bonferroni}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{clllcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Rank} & \\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{$p$-val} & \\textbf{Thresh.} & \\textbf{Pass} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1  & R1-8B  & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0042 & \\checkmark \\\\\n2  & OT-7B  & Bal.\\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0045 & \\checkmark \\\\",
      "numLines": 200,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 1211
    }
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0121oXbD9ZLWWy8eicgYcayT`</summary>

```text
955	\paragraph{Bootstrap CIs quantify statistical uncertainty.}
956	For the most fragile result (R1-8B FPR), bias-corrected accelerated (BCa) bootstrap~\citep{efron1993bootstrap} yields a 95\% CI of [0.006, 0.159] ($p{=}0.033$), with 12\% leave-one-out instability (\cref{app:multi_seed}). The CI excludes zero and confirms significance despite seed fragility. CP annotation perturbation (\cref{tab:cp_perturbation}) further shows that $\pm 2$-step label noise changes BA by ${<}2.5$pp, so annotation uncertainty does not compound with hyperparameter selection.
957	
958	\paragraph{Effective degrees of freedom.}
959	The full exploration space (5 metrics $\times$ 7 windows $\times$ 25--64 layers $=$ up to 2{,}240 configurations) far exceeds the 12 final HB-corrected tests. This does not constitute 2{,}240 independent hypothesis tests because layer and window are selected on the validation set before test-set evaluation---a standard train/validate/test protocol, not post-hoc cherry-picking on test outcomes. The effective degrees of freedom lie between 12 (final tests) and 2{,}240 (full grid), a ``garden of forking paths'' concern~\citep{gelman2013garden}. Three safeguards bound this risk: (1)~nested 5-fold CV on 7--8B models shows 13--25\% shrinkage relative to holdout estimates (\cref{app:nested_cv}), quantifying overfitting; (2)~split-half replication on OT-7B confirms the HS advantage on independent data halves (\cref{app:split_half}); (3)~threshold-based metric convergence to a narrow layer band (L63 for both 32B models) is inconsistent with scattered overfitting selections. We acknowledge that cumulative researcher degrees of freedom are substantial and that the 12-test HB boundary may underestimate Type~I error.
960	
961	\subsection{Nested Cross-Validation}
962	\label{app:nested_cv}
963	
964	The holdout estimates in \cref{tab:hs_vs_text} select layer and window on the validation fold, then evaluate on a fixed test set. To rule out hyperparameter overfitting, we run nested 5-fold cross-validation on both 7--8B models: the outer fold holds out test data; the inner loop selects (layer, $W$) on the remaining four folds. \cref{tab:nested_cv} reports per-fold precision differences.
```

```text
902	
903	For QwQ-32B specifically, three complementary significance tests confirm the residualized probe performs above chance: Wilcoxon signed-rank $p{=}0.0024$, paired $t$-test $p{=}0.0008$, permutation test $p{<}0.001$ (bootstrap 95\% CI for per-trace BA: $[0.575, 0.744]$). The position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\ HS 0.796), and HS precision (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) and FPR (+2.5pp, $p{=}0.279$) margins over the position baseline are not statistically significant at $n{=}19$.
904	
905	\paragraph{Non-linear position terms.}
906	To test whether non-linear position effects drive the HS advantage, we repeat the residualization with both linear ($t/T$) and quadratic ($[t/T, (t/T)^2]$) regressors (\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Both 7--8B models retain significant residualized advantages under either specification. R1-32B remains non-significant under both. QwQ-32B's residualized advantage survives linear control (+7.4pp, HB) but is fully absorbed by the quadratic term ($-$1.0pp), indicating a non-linear position component in its HS advantage.
```

_source line 96 · 2026-05-20T16:17:10.981Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016WJVNogsB8FscGcRoLHY1Y`</summary>

```text
69	\paragraph{Encoder robustness.} Against BGE-large~\citep{xiao2023cpack} (1024d), only OT-7B survives HB (1/4 precision); R1-8B shows adverse FPR direction (+1.2pp). The advantage is encoder-conditional (\cref{fig:forest_precision}); layer selection (Contribution 1) is encoder-independent.\looseness=-1
70	
71	\paragraph{Position mediation.} Position ($t/T$) accounts for 9--20pp of balanced accuracy,\footnote{R1-32B's 20.4pp drop reflects $W{=}1$; at $W{\geq}3$, drops are 9--17pp.} but all models retain non-positional signal above chance (all $p{<}0.001$; \cref{app:position_residual}). Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage (residualized gap +3.7 to +6.9pp, HB corrected); R1-32B's gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$, $n{=}20$). Adding a quadratic position term $(t/T)^2$ does not change significance conclusions for the 7--8B models. However, QwQ-32B's advantage is fully absorbed by the quadratic term (\cref{tab:position_quadratic}), suggesting its residualized advantage contains a non-linear position component. Temporal windowing reduces position dependence (24.3pp drop at $W{=}3$ vs.\ 16.5pp at $W{=}15$).\looseness=-1
72	
73	\paragraph{Caveats.} Nested 5-fold CV confirms the HS precision advantage for both 7--8B models (10/10 folds; \cref{app:nested_cv}), with shrinkage of 13--25\% relative to holdout estimates, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. OT-7B nested CV selects L21--27 vs.\ holdout L16 vs.\ CR-composite L0; the +21.9pp holdout estimate is an upper bound, and the nested CV shrunk estimate is the conservative reference. This inconsistency itself supports Contribution~1 (metric-induced layer selection bias). R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\% coverage;\footnote{R1-32B's BA-optimal layer was identified post-hoc rather than through the pre-registered nested CV protocol used for 7--8B models, further motivating its exploratory status.} at $W{\geq}3$, dimensionality prevents significance. Regularization $C \in [0.01, 100]$ yields ${<}2$pp BA variation; decision thresholds are similarly stable (\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}). QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps): higher precision at later detection. False alarm rates remain 11--50\%, so current methods characterize the phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors. Per-model details in \cref{sec:32b-analysis}.\looseness=-1
```

```text
565	\bottomrule
566	\end{tabular}}
567	\end{table}
568	
569	LLM annotators achieve only moderate agreement on jailbreak classification ($\kappa{=}0.606$) and low CP position consistency (37\% exact match). GPT-4o-mini is substantially more aggressive in jailbreak identification (99 vs.\ 52), suggesting systematic annotator bias. The low CP position agreement (mean absolute difference of 8.5 steps) supports the use of human-validated annotations in our primary analysis (HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts}) and motivates future investment in multi-annotator human CP labeling with formal IAA evaluation.
570	
571	\section{BOCPD/CUSUM Configuration Sweep}
572	\label{app:bocpd_config}
573	
574	We sweep hyperparameters for both Bayesian Online Change-Point Detection (BOCPD) and Cumulative Sum (CUSUM) methods applied to the temporal probe score sequences.
```

```text
645	\bottomrule
646	\end{tabular}}
647	\end{table}
648	
649	$K{=}1$ yields perfect coverage but near-perfect safe FPR (0.92--1.00), offering no discrimination. $K{=}5$ is empirically selected as a middle-ground tradeoff: average coverage 0.60 with average safe FPR 0.48 across models. OT-7B benefits most from increasing $K$, reaching 0.19 safe FPR at $K{=}5$ (vs.\ 0.92 at $K{=}1$) while retaining 51\% coverage. Higher $K$ values ($\geq$7) reduce safe FPR further but at steep coverage cost. $K{=}5$ vs.\ $K{=}7$ is the key decision: $K{=}7$ reduces mean safe FPR from 0.48 to 0.30 but drops mean coverage from 0.60 to 0.42.
650	
651	$K{=}5$ was selected on the R1-8B validation set and held fixed across all models; main-text results use this value without per-model optimization. The $K \times W$ interaction is not fully explored: this ablation uses $W{=}1$ to isolate $K$'s effect, and the joint $K \times W$ surface remains a limitation. At optimal $W$, coverage at $K{=}5$ improves (e.g., R1-8B: 0.47 at $W{=}1$ vs.\ 0.60 at $W{=}15$).
652	
653	\subsection{Deployment Operating Points}
654	\label{app:deployment_operating_points}
```

```text
2. appendix.tex:569 - "suggesting systematic annotator bias" — another `-ing` clause at the end.

Now I'm tracking down em-dashes across the paper's sections. Now I'm tallying up all the em-dashes I've found across the paper: abstract has 2, introduction has 3, related work has 2, experiments has at least 1, and appendix has 2, bringing the total to around 10 em-dashes with roughly 8 in the main text. Since the writing norms require removing all of them, this is a substantial revision task. I'm ready to compile the final audit report now, though I should also verify whether the paper includes a Future Work section.
```

```
[CONTENT_AUDIT_START]

=== 叙事一致性（CRITICAL 维度） ===

- [warning] [叙事一致性] abstract.tex:1 术语漂移：Abstract 定义 "commitment point (CP)"，Intro/Method 定义 "Safety Commitment Point (CP)"，全称不一致，应统一为 "Safety Commitment Point"

- [warning] [叙事一致性] abstract.tex:1 Abstract 提及 ensemble 结果 "A three-layer ensemble provides metric-agnostic HS advantage matching the best individual metric (4/4 models, p≤0.002)"，但此结果未出现在 Intro 贡献列表中。Abstract 中突出展示的结果应与贡献列表对齐，或降级为一句话提及

- [info] [叙事一致性] 整体叙事链完整：Abstract→Intro→Method→Experiments→Discussion→Conclusion 均围绕同一主线（自越狱动机→probe方法论问题→layer selection bias→HS advantage条件性），三条 Contribution 均有对应 section/table 支撑，无严重漂移

=== 章节结构 ===

- [warning] [章节结构] abstract.tex:1 Abstract 技术密度过高：第3句起即进入 crossing-rate composite metric、384d encoder、Holm-Bonferroni correction 等未定义术语，审稿人30秒内无法判断核心价值。建议：前3句讲清问题-方法-核心发现（layer selection bias），后面压缩数字细节

- [warning] [章节结构] abstract.tex:1 Abstract 中 "upper bound" 缺少上下文解释（指 holdout estimate 相对 nested CV 的 shrinkage），读者需到正文才能理解此括注含义

- [warning] [章节结构] conclusion.tex Conclusion 仅3行，缺少任何前瞻性总结或 future directions。Discussion §Guidance for future research 有此内容但 Conclusion 未引用。建议补充1-2句展望（causal validation、跨 benchmark 验证等）

- [warning] [章节结构] related_work.tex:13 "Hidden-State Safety Detection" 段落过长（~250 words），引用密度极高（>20 citations），部分区域接近 "A does X; B does Y" 堆砌模式（尤其 TrajGuard→SafeSwitch→SIREN→SafeDream→HiddenDetect→SafeInt→RCS 连续列举）。建议按功能子主题（如 token-level vs trajectory-level）分组

=== 贡献-范围对齐 ===

- [info] [贡献范围] 三条 Contribution 全部有 section/table 支撑：C1→§4.2+Tab2+Fig1+App:random_cp; C2→§4.3+Tab3+Fig2; C3→§4.6+Tab:negative。无孤儿章节

- [info] [贡献范围] Title "Layer Selection Sensitivity" 聚焦 C1（方法论主贡献），C2/C3 作为辅助贡献，标题范围与内容匹配

- [info] [贡献范围] Abstract/Intro 明确声明 "primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models)"，范围词诚实，未过度泛化

=== Claim 强度匹配 ===

- [info] [Claim强度] Claim 标定极其审慎：使用三级 evidence gradient（cross-validated/significant/exploratory），所有 32B 结果标 †（sample-limited），+21.9pp 标注为 upper bound（nested CV 有 13-25% shrinkage）。无发现过强表述

- [info] [Claim强度] 未使用 "state-of-the-art"/"significantly outperforms" 等高强度词。"significantly" 仅在 appendix.tex:1209 出现1次（统计语境，acceptable）

=== 实验严谨性 ===

- [info] [实验严谨性] 主结果表（Tab 3）报告 BCa bootstrap 95% CI（10,000 resamples），multi-seed 稳定性在 appendix（18/20 model-seed pass HB）。Nested CV 提供 shrinkage 估计。统计框架充分

- [info] [实验严谨性] 7种 baseline（BOCPD, CUSUM, Mahalanobis, RepE, MLP, CCS, cross-model transfer），覆盖 change-point detection、representation engineering、transferability 多个方向。TrajGuard 有复现

- [info] [实验严谨性] Ablation 覆盖全面：layer sweep、window size、K threshold、encoder quality（3 encoders）、capacity control（PCA/random projection）、position mediation（linear+quadratic）

=== 可复现性披露 ===

- [info] [可复现性] 超参完整（C=1.0, LBFGS, max_iter=2000, K=5, W selection, split 60/20/20）。GPU: "under 4 GPU-hours on a single RTX A6000"。ARR Responsible NLP Research Checklist 已填写（App A）。Ethics Statement 存在

=== 符号/术语一致性 ===

- [warning] [符号一致性] "CP" 全称不统一：abstract.tex 用 "commitment point"，introduction.tex/method.tex 用 "Safety Commitment Point"。全文应统一为后者

- [info] [符号一致性] 核心符号（$y_t$, $s_t$, $\mathbf{h}_t^{(\ell)}$, $W$, $K$, CR-composite, $\dagger$）首次出现均有定义，跨章节使用一致

=== 章节过渡与逻辑流 ===

- [info] [章节过渡] Discussion 提供高层解读（几何机制、encoder boundary、future guidance、ensemble proof-of-concept），未简单复述 Results 数字。引用表格编号而非重复数值

- [info] [章节过渡] Method→Experiments 有 forward reference（"Results in §4.2"）。Experiments 各 subsection 之间有逻辑递进（layer selection→modality comparison→32B details→temporal→negative）

=== Limitations 诚实度 ===

- [info] [Limitations] 4段 ~500 words，覆盖：单数据集限制（+IAA缺失）、model-specific caveats（FPR 11-50%、power analysis）、hyperparameter selection（garden of forking paths）、CP permanence assumption。实质性极强，无回避式表述

=== 去 AI 风格 ===

- [warning] [去AI] abstract.tex:1 LaTeX em-dash (---) 用于解释性插入语："the commitment point (CP)---the first reasoning step...---requires monitoring"。应改为逗号或括号

- [warning] [去AI] introduction.tex:5 em-dash："conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer..."。应改为冒号或句号分句

- [warning] [去AI] introduction.tex:7 em-dash 对："four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based...---at two scales"。应改为括号

- [warning] [去AI] related_work.tex:13 em-dash 对："a distinct phenomenon---commitment transitions during reasoning---that is absent"。应改为括号或逗号

- [warning] [去AI] experiments.tex:13 em-dash（具体位置在长段落内）

- [warning] [去AI] appendix.tex:79, 959 em-dash（附录）

- [info] [去AI] experiments.tex:71 "-ing" 分词结尾："suggesting its residualized advantage contains a non-linear position component"。可改为 "which suggests..."

- [info] [去AI] appendix.tex:569 "-ing" 结尾："suggesting systematic annotator bias"

- [info] [去AI] appendix.tex:649 "-ing" 结尾："offering no discrimination"

- [info] [去AI] appendix.tex:906 "-ing" 结尾："indicating a non-linear position component"

- [info] [去AI] 无 "Furthermore/Moreover/Additionally" 连续使用，无 "It is worth noting/Notably"，无 "delve/leverage/utilize"，无过度分段。整体 AI 痕迹控制良好

[CONTENT_AUDIT_END]
```

**审计摘要**：

- **叙事一致性**：核心叙事链完整，无严重漂移。两个 warning：CP 术语全称不统一、ensemble 结果在 Abstract 突出但不在贡献列表中
- **最突出问题**：正文 5 处 + 附录 2 处 LaTeX em-dash (`---`)，需全部替换为逗号/括号/分句
- **Abstract 可读性**：技术密度过高，30 秒内难以判断核心价值
- **Conclusion 过短**：仅 3 行，缺前瞻性内容
- **Related Work 密度**：Hidden-State Safety Detection 段落过长，引用堆砌倾向
- **Claim 标定**：极其审慎，三级 evidence gradient + nested CV + HB 校正，无过强表述
- **Limitations**：500+ words，4 段，实质性极强

[AUDIT_DONE]
